关于基孔肯雅热和登革热的常见问题 作者:Loukrakpam Bina - E-Pao.net

Common things about Chikungunya and Dengue By Loukrakpam Bina - E-Pao.net
新闻简报基孔肯雅热和登革热是热带地区流行的蚊媒病毒性疾病,特别是在坦桑尼亚南部、曼尼普尔邦和南美洲。由于共享蚊子 媒介 和气候变化,同时出现的 暴发 正在上升。这两种疾病的特征都是发烧、关节疼痛和皮疹。混合感染的患者可能会出现更严重的临床并发症,新生儿可能会出现长期残疾。公共卫生措施包括 监测、媒介 控制和预防策略。
查看新闻五要素
时间
AIR Radio Live 官方目录 电台 E-Pao YellOut 日历 eRang Ooba 视频 eMing 书籍 eYek 新闻评论 曼尼普尔邦旅游教育特色 画廊 休闲链接 Mayek 全景工具 贡献电子邮件 今天 - 星期二,2026 年 11 月 10 日 ✦ Ask AI 你在这里:首页 » 教育 » 健康问题 关于基孔肯雅热和登革热的常见问题 Dr Loukrakpam Bina Chanui * 登革热:: Pix - TSE 热带地区由节肢动物传播的病毒引起的蚊媒病毒性疾病是通过受感染的雌性蚊子叮咬传播给人类的感染,主要由伊蚊和库蚊传播。
地点与对象
坦桑尼亚南部、曼尼普尔邦、巴西、巴拉圭、秘鲁、阿根廷、哥伦比亚、印度。
事件
基孔肯雅热和登革热是由伊蚊传播的蚊媒病毒性疾病。这些疾病的特点是发烧、关节疼痛和其他症状。由于共享蚊子 媒介 和气候变化,同时出现的 暴发 正在上升。
规模、影响与风险
基孔肯雅热和登革热在热带地区流行。重合 暴发 与高烧、皮疹和身体疼痛有关。合并感染的患者可能会出现更严重的临床并发症。新生儿可能会出现长期残疾。这些疾病通过蚊虫叮咬传播,在人口稠密的城市地区风险更高。
应对、状态与不确定性
这些疾病正在调查中。公共卫生措施包括 监测、媒介 控制和预防策略。所提供的证据中没有报告确切的病例和死亡人数。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Common things about Chikungunya and Dengue By Loukrakpam Bina - E-Pao.net

News Brief
Chikungunya and Dengue are mosquito-borne viral diseases prevalent in tropical regions, particularly in southern Tanzania, Manipur, and South America. Coinciding outbreaks are rising due to shared mosquito vectors and climate change. Both diseases are characterized by fever, joint pain, and rash. Coinfected patients may experience harsher clinical complications, and neonates may develop long-term disabilities. Public health measures include surveillance, vector control, and prevention strategies.
Original source text (English)
AIR Radio Live Officials Directory Radio E-Pao YellOut Calendar eRang Ooba Video eMing Books eYek News Reviews Manipur Travel Education Features Galleries Leisure Links Mayek Panorama Tools Contribute eMail us TODAY - Tuesday, Nov 10, 2026 ✦ Ask AI You are here: Home » education » Health Issue Common things about Chikungunya and Dengue Dr Loukrakpam Bina Chanui * Dengue :: Pix - TSE Mosquito-borne viral diseases in tropical regions due to arthropod-borne viruses are infections transmitted through the bites of infected female mosquitoes to humans, mainly by Aedes and Culex. Dengue is the most prevalent viral infection transmitted by Aedes mosquitoes (like Aedes aegypti) where infected persons are associated with high fever, severe joint and muscle pain, and headache, with an estimated 96 million symptomatic cases globally each year. Chikungunya is also one of the mosquito-borne viral disease transmitted through the bite of female Aedes species, characterized by sudden fever and debilitating, long-lasting joint pain (arthralgia). Aedes mosquitoes are active during the daytime and are identifiable by distinct black-and-white mar- kings. Their breeding grounds are stagnant, pooled water to hatch eggs, while warmer tropical temperatures accelerating their reproduction and viral replication rates. Chikungunya is a disease of fever and severe joint pain. The disease outbreak occurred in southern Tanzania during 1952. Female Aedes species responsible for Chikungunya virus (CHIKV) were Aedes aegypti and Aedes albopic-tus, which can also transmit Dengue and Zika viruses. They are daytime biter. Chikungunya may be acute, sub-acute and chronic. Though death is rare due to severity of symptoms, young children and old aged patients are at higher risk for severe disease. Sudden onset of fever accompanied by arthritis-like symptoms with varied duration, neurological complications as Guillain-Barré syndrome and meningo-encephalitis are characteristics of the disease. The infection is recoverable and provide lifelong immunity. Infants are also affected by chikungunya. The CHIKV virus is not transmitted to the foetus during pregnancy. But there are 49% cases of transmission to babies if women are infected during the intrapartum period through vertical transmission. Infected babies are associated with neurologic diseases such as meningo-encephalitis, white matter lesions, cerebral oedema, and intracranial haemorrhage and myocardial disease. Raised liver function tests, reduced platelet and lymphocyte counts, and decreased prothrombin levels are often detected during laboratory diagnosis. Neonates sometimes develop long-term disabilities. Coinciding outbreaks of Chikungunya (CHIKV) and Dengue (DENV) are rising significantly, driven by shared mosquito vectors (Aedes aegypti and Aedes albopictus), rapid urbanization, and expanding geographic ranges linked to climate change. Because these viruses are transmitted by the same mosquito species, a single mosquito bite can transmit both pathogens, leading to simultaneous seasonal epidemics and a growing rate of clinical co-infections. An alarming surge of simultaneous arboviral transmi- ssion has been highlighted by the World Health Organization (WHO). In Brazil, Paraguay, Peru, Argentina, and Colombia severe coinciding outbreaks have been witnessed. The expansion of CHIKV transmission far beyond its historical boundaries have confirmed by the WHO Risk Assessment where the two diseases directly overlap in the dengue hyper- endemic areas. There were case study reports from Brazil where patients with common symptoms were confirmed infected by DENV and CHIKV after laboratory diagnosis during the overlapping epidemic waves during 2013- 2020. Recent research outlining the Dengue-Chikungunya Interface in South Asia shows that both viruses are consistently peaking during the same post-monsoon months. A global systematic review published in Acta Tropica during the year 2022, through Statistical Meta Analysis established that Asia accounts for the highest global prevalence of DENV-CHIKV co-infections at 3.3%. From the India Outbreak Data, multi-year surveillance data from central and northern India shows high concurrent positivity rates. A study in Bhopal (2022–2023) found that out of thousands of suspected cases, 23.86% had dengue, 13.30% had chikungunya, and 6.55% were actively co-infected with both. Similarly, data from Punjab recorded a co-detection rate of 9.1% among tested patients during coinciding outbreaks. In Manipur, the prevalence of Dengue and Chikungunya is highly concentrated in the low-lying valley regions, though recent ecological shifts have caused them to emerge in certain hill districts as well. According to data from the Directorate of Health Services (DHS), Manipur, with most heavily affected areas include: The urban and peri-urban districts of the Imphal Valley bear the vast majority of the State’s arboviral disease burden due to high population densities and rapid urban development. Imphal West is consistently the worst-hit district in the state. High-density municipal blocks, construction zones, and areas like Ching-meirong regularly record the highest percentages of seasonal infections. Imphal East is the second most affected valley zone. Urban cluster areas adjacent to major medical centres like JNIMS face significant seasonal surges. Thoubal and Bishnupur districts experience widespread transmission, particularly in residential pockets with open drainage infrastructure and localized farming pools. While hilly terrains historically had fewer cases due to cooler temperatures, vector migration has introduced the diseases to new sectors. Churachandpur district has emerged as a major focus area. Surveillance reports have flagged Churachandpur town for sharp multi-disease outbreaks, with its hospital networks treating a substantial volume of pediatric and adult febrile cases. Other Hill Districts like Chandel, Ukhrul, and Senapati report fewer total numbers than the central valley but consistently log active, low-level transmission during the warm post-monsoon months. Valley Districts of Manipur such as Imphal West, Imphal East, Thoubal, have high human population density that can create rapid transmission chains. The areas have trapped ambient heat in concrete areas forming localized ‘heat islands’. So, Hyper-localized outbreaks where a high volume of mosquitoes interact with dense human popula-tions is possible. New frontier areas in hill districts with changing land-use patterns and deforestation invite vector migration. Hilly zones that were historically too cold are now warm enough to support Aedes survival. Spillover zones where communities with little to no historical immunity face sudden outbreaks is suggested now-a- days. Aedes mosquitoes shared vector competence where it can carry and transmit DENV and CHIKV simultaneously. In the areas where there is rapid urbanisation, drainage deficits, and poor water management systems, the mosquitoes create their ideal breeding grounds. Virus like the CHIKV E1-A226V variant undergo rapid genetic mutations allowing the virus efficiently adapt and spread to new areas through Aedes albopictus. When there are coinciding outbreaks, both illnesses are associated with high fever, rash, and body aches creating diagnostic confusions. However, harsher clinical complications including significantly higher rates of leucopenia (low white blood cell count) and hypokalemia (low potassium) will occur in a co-infected patient as compared to patients suffering from just one of the viruses. If, dual outbreaks occur, the public may confuse the two diseases due to overlapping symptoms like sudden high fever, headache, and rash. If there is timely communication between the public and health care workers, one could clearly differentiate the distinct long-term risks of each virus. Awareness campaigns should teach the public that while Dengue requires urgent monitoring for life- threatening bleeding risks (due to dropping platelets). Chikungunya causes severe, long-lasting joint pain that requires different long-term management but rarely causes haemorrhagic shock. Integrated education should warn citizens against taking non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) to overcome joint pain before confirming it isn’t Dengue. Taking NSAIDs during a Dengue infection can trigger severe internal bleeding. Integrated messaging guides should teach people to control the fever as the first-line of response. Separate health campaigns for different mos- quito-borne diseases may confuse communities and dilute the impact of prevention messages. Integrated awareness based on One Vector, One Solution should unite the public under a single, highly actionable strategies. Because Aedes mosquitoes bite during the day and breed in clean, stagnant water, the prevention message is identical. Integrated campaigns will simplify community action into a single preventive measure: source reduction (scrubbing containers, emptying flowerpots), installing screens, and wearing daytime repellent. Giving the Public about the awareness Dengue, and Chikungunya may lead to information overload. ‘Aedes Defense’ campaign under a single comprehensive initiative can keep public engagement high. During simultaneous outbreaks, health departments face severe funding, logis-tical, and staffing cons- traints. Integrated awareness can create immense operational efficiency. Producing a single set of educational materials, awareness booklet, and community work- shops can lower costs of intervention efforts. Frontline health workers can distribute comprehensive toolkits covering both diseases during a single household visit, dramatically increasing the geographic reach and speed of public health outreach. Integrated awareness turns citizens into active public health data collectors. When the public understands the distinct symptoms of both viruses (e.g., the signature severe joint swelling of Chikungunya versus the retro-orbital eye pain of Dengue), their reports to local clinics become much more accurate. This high-quality community data allows epidemiologists to quickly map out precisely where co-circulation is happening, enabling rapid, targeted vector control in high-risk neighbourhoods. In Manipur, arboviral infections exhibit a strict seasonal pattern. The transmission starts during June–September of Southwest Monsoon months, and reach its peak during the post-monsoon period of October–November. The geographic terrain of Manipur particularly the low-lying Imphal Valley is a distinct ecological pocket where heavy rainfall directly accelerates mosquito breeding grounds. Recent spatial and epidemiological modelling have published in Geospatial Insights into Dengue in Manipur used climate datasets (CHIRPS precipitation and MODIS temperature) to trace outbreak patterns. The state experiences its heaviest rainfall from June to September. Epidemiological data shows a strong positive correlation (0.81 to 0.82) between specific climate markers (high relative humidity, moderate tempera- ture fluctuations, and monsoon precipitation) and confirmed serological cases. Heavy monsoon rains initially wash away larvae, but as the rains recede into October as post-monsoon lag, thousands of pockets of stagnant water remain. Due to the combined post-monsoon ambient warmth, creates the perfect breeding ground for Aedes aegypti and Aedes albopictus. A multi-year study tracking arboviral trends through the Viral Research and Diagnostic Laboratories (VRDL) network in Manipur established definitive proof of this timeline that - Out of thousands of suspected cases analysed via NS1 and IgM ELISA, the vast majority of positive samples were concentrated between June and October. The peak shifted toward late September and October, proving that the post-monsoon climate facilitates hyper-transmission. Historically, Manipur’s cooler hilly climate restricted the survival of tropical vectors like the Aedes mosquito. However, shifts in climatic conditions have broken these natural barriers. The Indian Meteorological Department (IMD) recorded Manipur’s summer temperatures as hitting up to 38°C in recent years. The IMD data has showed the strong correlation between rising temperatures and exponential case increases of dengue with a rate of 5,519 cases documented in 2025 alone. Prolonged Seasons due to High Humidity: According to public health observations by the Manipur Health Services Directorate have a keen observation of the public health, and showed that shifting rainfall patterns have created prolonged high humidity seasons which are ideal microclimates that keep mosquito populations active late long into November, after the traditional monsoon seasons. The extrinsic incubation period of the viruses within the mosquito vector decreases in the warmed mountain valleys leading them bite more frequently. During 3-7 days of infection, symptoms of both diseases are nearly identical. Most of the infected patients show - Sudden onset of high-grade fever (often reaching 102°F–104°F). As the fever spikes up, maculopapular skin rashes appear primarily on the trunk and limbs. The shared symptoms of both the diseases are severe body aches, muscle pain, generalized joint pain, intense, debilitating frontal headaches accompanied by profound fatigue and nausea. Distinct pathognomonic features can help clinicians differentiate between the two diseases. Blood parameters must be monitored closely to prevent mortality in case of dengue infection. Severe pain behind the eyes worsened by eye movement could be encountered which is a hallmark for dengue. Bleeding gums, epistaxis (nosebleeds), petechiae (purple skin spots), and internal bleeding may develop in the patients with severe cases of dengue which symptoms are collectively known as Dengue Hemor-rhagic Fever (DHF). Sudden drop in blood pressure, organ failure, and a dangerous plunge in platelet counts (thrombo- cytopenia) may happen due to critical plasma leakage. Though Chikungunya exhibits severe, crippling musculoskeletal morbidity it rarely causes life-threatening shock. Dengue causes transient joint aches while intense, localized joint pain around the wrists, ankles, and small joints of the hands are associated with Chikungunya. The pain is associated with visible symptoms like joint swelling, severe stiffness and inflammation just like in Arthritis patients. Dengue may clear within weeks but Chikungunya’s joint pain proceeds into a chronic phase with arthritis-like symptoms persisting for months to years. Community anxiety and dangerous medical practices may be triggered due to the similar and identical initial symptoms if co-circulation of the two distinct viruses occur. As joint pain is a dominant feature of both illnesses, individuals may bypass crowded hospitals and relies at the over-the-counter painkillers from local pharmacies. Taking traditional NSAIDs (like ibuprofen) assuming to be Chikungunya can lead to severe bleeding complications of Dengue. Cases may continue to rise due to the dual viral presence even though control teams conduct fogging operations. Public frustration can mount leading to the misunderstandings why some patients recover in a week (classic Dengue/Chikun-gunya) while others suffer joint pain for months (chronic Chikungunya). Local rumours and anxiety may overload. There may be Community confusion regarding the severity of symptoms. Critical Dengue warning signs (such as persistent vomiting or abdominal pain) may be mistaken for typical Chikungunya fatigue. There may be delay in seeking hospital care, with patients often arriving at emergency wards only after advanced shock is progressing. Public health frameworks in Manipur must shift away from disease-specific silos, because Dengue and Chikungunya share identical mosquito vectors. Reinforcing one vector, one solution is an integrated strategy to be promoted. Community mobilisation targeting the Aedes mosquito will protect households from both viruses simultaneously. Personal Protection is also one solution to be taken up at individual level. Aedes aegypti and Aedes albopictus are strictly active during the daytime, have peak biting hours at dawn and dusk. Only sleeping under a mosquito net will offer no protection against daytime bites. We should emphasise the used of mosquito nets for infants and the elderly, while of insecticide-treated mosquito nets (ITNs) for the shift-workers who sleep or rest indoors during daylight hours. Insect repellents like DEET, Picaridin, or oil of lemon eucalyptus applications are effective in protecting ourself from the mosquitoes during outdoor works. Students attending daytime classes and vendors working in open-air shifts at bustling commercial hubs like Ima Keithel should also use these repellents. Wearing light-coloured, long- sleeved shirts and full-length trousers can create a highly effective physical barrier against aggressive vector bites. Cutting off the Aedes mosquito life cycle by eliminating any accessible standing water where female mosquitoes deposit their eggs is best environmental management stra- tegy. Weekly ‘Dry Day’ (e.g., every Sunday) Campaign should be syn- chronized in every local and places and institutes. Every household, school, and Government office in Manipur should completely empty, scrub, and dry all water storage containers, flower pots, coolers, and terrace trays on this day. Many residents routinely store water in large plastic drums, sintex tanks, and traditional metal vessels due to the intermittent municipal water supply in many parts of Imphal valley. These containers should be kept tightly covered with lids or fine mesh screens to prevent female mosquitoes from accessing the water. Local municipal authorities should take the active responsibility to clear blocked roadside drains, open construction ditches, and scattered plastic wastes, as heavy monsoon downpours will turn these...

Location and population
Southern Tanzania, Manipur, Brazil, Paraguay, Peru, Argentina, Colombia, India.
Event
Chikungunya and Dengue are mosquito-borne viral diseases transmitted by Aedes mosquitoes. The diseases are characterized by fever, joint pain, and other symptoms. Coinciding outbreaks are rising due to shared mosquito vectors and climate change.
Scale, impact and risk
Chikungunya and Dengue are prevalent in tropical regions. Coinciding outbreaks are associated with high fever, rash, and body aches. Co-infected patients may experience harsher clinical complications. Neonates may develop long-term disabilities. The diseases are transmitted through mosquito bites, and the risk is higher in densely populated urban areas.
Response, status and uncertainty
The diseases are under investigation. Public health measures include surveillance, vector control, and prevention strategies. The exact number of cases and deaths is not reported in the supplied evidence.

瑞士出现首例病例——以下是您需要了解的有关 基孔肯雅热 的信息 - blue News

First Cases in Switzerland—Here's What You Need to Know About Chikungunya Fever - blue News
新闻简报瑞士首例 基孔肯雅病毒 感染确诊病例出现在提契诺州,特别是贝林佐纳附近的马加迪诺平原。提契诺州卫生当局证实,住在同一条街上的四人通过虎蚊叮咬在当地感染了该病毒。这些感染被归类为本土感染,这意味着它们是在国内传播的。该病毒主要通过虎蚊传播,虎蚊原产于热带和亚热带地区,但自 2000 年代初以来一直存在于瑞士。症状包括高烧、严重关节和肌肉疼痛以及头痛,目前尚无有效药物。联邦公共卫生办公室 (FOPH) 和提契诺州卫生当局表示,感染风险仍然较低,部分原因是病例数量有限且蚊子季节即将结束。当地正在采取措施控制剩余的蚊子数量,并建议公众采取预防措施,例如使用驱蚊剂和穿长衣服。

时间
据 N12 报道,发布日期为 2026 年 11 月 5 日,事件日期据信发生在夏末。
地点与对象
如 N11 中所述,首例确诊的本地感染是在瑞士提契诺州发现的,特别是在贝林佐纳附近的马加迪诺平原。据 N13 报道,受影响的人是住在同一条街上的四人。
事件
据《提契诺邮报》报道并 SUPSI 在 N18 中引用,瑞士提契诺州卫生当局确认了四例本地获得的基孔肯雅热感染病例。
规模、影响与风险
这些感染被认为是本土感染,这意味着它们是通过蚊子在当地传播的,但感染的风险仍然很低。该疾病通常较轻,症状包括高烧、严重关节和肌肉疼痛以及头痛,如 N23 和 N24 中所述。目前尚无有效药物,只能使用退烧止痛药物来缓解症状,如N32和N33所述。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

First Cases in Switzerland—Here's What You Need to Know About Chikungunya Fever - blue News

News Brief
The first confirmed cases of chikungunya virus infections in Switzerland were reported in Ticino, specifically in the Magadino Plain near Bellinzona. Four individuals, all living on the same street, contracted the virus locally through tiger mosquito bites, as confirmed by Ticino health authorities. The infections are classified as autochthonous, meaning they were transmitted within the country. The virus is primarily spread by tiger mosquitoes, which are native to tropical and subtropical regions but have been present in Switzerland since the early 2000s. Symptoms include high fever, severe joint and muscle pain, and headaches, with no effective medication available. The Federal Office of Public Health (FOPH) and Ticino health authorities have stated that the risk of infection remains low, partly due to the limited number of cases and the approaching end of the mosquito season. Local measures are being implemented to control the remaining mosquito population, and the public is advised to take preventive actions such as using mosquito repellent and wearing long clothing.

Start Sport Switzerland International Football People MyTech Lifestyle Start Sport Switzerland International Football People MyTech Lifestyle LifestyleWhat You Need to Know About Chikungunya Fever A Treacherous Tropical Virus First Cases in Switzerland—Here's What You Need to Know About Chikungunya Fever 02:02 Kampf gegen die Tigermücke: USA setzen auf Bakterien – die Schweiz auf sterile Mückenmännchen For the first time, four people in Switzerland have been infected with the chikungunya virus. The virus is transmitted by the tiger mosquito. How dangerous is the disease, what symptoms does it cause, and how can you protect yourself? Britta Gfeller 05.11.2026, 12:42•05.11.2026, 15:21 Here's what it's all about The chikungunya virus is transmitted by tiger mosquitoes. Local infections have been detected in Switzerland for the first time, but the risk of infection remains low. Typical symptoms of chikungunya include high fever, severe joint and muscle pain, and headaches. The disease is usually mild, but joint pain can last for months. The best ways to protect against chikungunya are mosquito repellent, long clothing, and mosquito nets. In Switzerland, a vaccine is also approved and recommended for travel to areas currently experiencing outbreaks. Summary created with In Ticino, there have been the first confirmed cases of people have been infected with the chikungunya virus within Switzerland. On Wednesday, Ticino health authorities confirmed four locally acquired infections in the Magadino Plain near Bellinzona. The infections are believed to have occurred at the end of summer. According to the Ticino University of Applied Sciences (SUPSI), the four affected individuals live on the same street. How is the virus transmitted? The virus is transmitted through mosquito bites, primarily those of the tiger mosquito. This species is native to the tropical and subtropical regions of South and Southeast Asia, but has also spread to many other regions of the world. It has been present in Switzerland since the early 2000s. The infections in Ticino are so-called “autochthonous cases,” as reported by the Corriere del Ticino reports, citing SUPSI. This means that a mosquito found in Ticino bit a person who had previously been infected in a high-risk area and had subsequently returned to Switzerland. The mosquito picked up the virus and transmitted it to other people through subsequent bites. The disease cannot be transmitted directly from person to person. What are the symptoms? According to the Federal Office of Public Health (FOPH), the disease usually develops seven to nine days after being bitten by an infected mosquito. Symptoms include high fever, severe joint and muscle pain, and headaches. Sometimes a rash also develops. How dangerous is the virus? According to the BAG, infection with the chikungunya virus is generally not dangerous. In rare cases, however, it can lead to prolonged fatigue and debilitating joint pain lasting weeks or months. In some cases, severe symptoms have been observed in newborns, older adults, and people with chronic illnesses. According to the Corriere del Ticino, the four people who contracted the virus in Switzerland are recovering well. Aside from persistent joint pain, they are doing fine. To date, there is no effective medication. Symptoms can only be relieved with fever-reducing and pain-relieving medications. Are we in for a pandemic like COVID-19? No. The Department of Health and Social Affairs of the Canton of Ticino has stated that the current risk to the public is considered very low, partly due to the low number of cases and the fact that the mosquito season is coming to an end. Local measures are being taken to control the remaining mosquitoes. The FOPH also states: “Individual local transmissions do not indicate a nationwide spread.” However, isolated cases could occur during mosquito season. How can you protect yourself? According to the FOPH, protection against mosquito bites remains the most important preventive measure, especially when traveling to affected areas. Important precautions include, for example, insect repellent, long-sleeved clothing, and mosquito nets at night. Chikungunya fever is endemic in numerous countries in Africa, India, and the Pacific. The virus has also become established in Central America, the Caribbean, some South American countries, and certain states in the southern United States. Cases have also been reported in Europe, with Italy and France reporting major outbreaks in recent years. Anyone who notices symptoms after returning from a high-risk area should inform their family doctor about the trip and continue to protect themselves from mosquito bites. According to the FOPH, a vaccine against chikungunya has been approved in Switzerland since April. According to the Swiss Expert Committee on Travel Medicine, the vaccine is recommended for travelers to areas currently experiencing chikungunya outbreaks. 01:47 Schädlinge in der Schweiz: Diese invasiven Arten bedrohen Pflanzen, Tier – und Mensch Von der Asiatischen Tigermücke bis hin zum Goldfisch – Diese Schädlinge können Krankheiten auf den Menschen übertragen, das Ökosystem schädigen und neben Wildpflanzen auch Kulturpflanzen befallen. B routine priorityPublic-health news

"The chikungunya cases in Ticino are a wake-up call for Switzerland"

News Brief
In Ticino, Switzerland, four individuals have tested positive for chikungunya fever, with two cases identified in October and one previously symptomatic in August. The cantonal medical officer, Giorgio Merlani, emphasizes the need for preventive measures against the Asian tiger mosquito, which also transmits dengue fever. All four patients are recovering well without hospitalization, and the current public risk is low due to winter conditions and local mosquito control efforts. However, Merlani warns that the virus may return with larger outbreaks in the future. The response includes spraying to eliminate adult mosquitoes, releasing sterile males, and monitoring larvae. A surveillance program for the West Nile virus is also underway. Merlani urges the federal government to support preventive actions and highlights the potential for dengue fever cases, which he considers more dangerous than chikungunya. The cases serve as a wake-up call for Switzerland to prepare for future outbreaks.

Start Sport Switzerland International Football People MyTech Lifestyle Start Sport Switzerland International Football People MyTech Lifestyle "The chikungunya cases in Ticino are a wake-up call for Switzerland" Found an error? Report now Tiger mosquito "The chikungunya cases in Ticino are a wake-up call for Switzerland" The Ticino cantonal medical officer is calling for preventive measures against the Asian tiger mosquito, as this species also transmits dengue fever. (File photo) Keystone At the moment, the risk to the public posed by the chikungunya virus is low, says Ticino’s cantonal medical officer Giorgio Merlani in an interview with the Keystone-SDA news agency. However, Switzerland will need to be prepared for the future. Keystone-SDA 07.11.2026, 09:01 Keystone-SDA: Giorgio Merlani, when did the four people affected by chikungunya fever test positive? Giorgio Merlani: “In mid-October, the chikungunya virus was detected in the blood of the patients. One of the four people had already experienced symptoms consistent with the virus in August but did not get tested. When the doctor encountered two other positive cases of the chikungunya virus in October, he recalled this case and tested that person as well.” Why is that? Do they all have the same doctor? Merlani: “Two of the four people who fell ill are related. The other two live nearby.” How are the four patients doing now? Did they have to be hospitalized? Merlani: “Fortunately, all four are doing quite well. They only had to go to the hospital for tests. Two of them mainly had the typical rash, while the other two mainly suffered from body aches.” Do you expect more locally transmitted cases to occur in the coming weeks? Merlani: “No, I don’t expect that to happen right now. But you should never say ‘never.’ There are only a few mosquitoes around at the moment. In addition, in collaboration with the University of Applied Sciences and Arts of Southern Switzerland (SUPSI), we’ve sprayed a substance in the residential area where those affected live that kills adult tiger mosquitoes. Furthermore, not a single tiger mosquito in Ticino has tested positive for the chikungunya virus so far.” Is it possible to kill the mosquitoes in question using a substance sprayed locally? Merlani: “Yes, that’s very effective, because a tiger mosquito flies a maximum of about 100 meters in its entire lifetime. Thanks to this measure and the onset of winter, the risk of contracting the chikungunya virus is low at the moment. But the outlook for the future is different. We need to be prepared. The four cases in Ticino are a wake-up call for Switzerland.” In your opinion, what measures will be necessary in the future? Merlani: “In addition to the usual, individual preventive measures against the proliferation of tiger mosquitoes, municipalities must also fulfill their responsibilities. In Ticino, some are already collaborating with SUPSI to release sterile male tiger mosquitoes. In addition, under SUPSI’s leadership, efforts are being made to control tiger mosquito larvae. Numerous egg-laying traps distributed throughout the canton also map the density, activity periods, and spread patterns of the mosquitoes. All of this has proven to be effective. However, caution is also advised for travelers arriving from areas with many cases of the chikungunya virus.” What should be done about this? Merlani: “If vacationers test positive for the chikungunya virus, they must cover themselves so that tiger mosquitoes cannot bite them. This is because the mosquitoes transmit the virus to other people after about a week.” Is Ticino once again a “gateway” for a new virus? Merlani: “Not necessarily; tiger mosquitoes are also present in German-speaking and French-speaking Switzerland. The four cases in Ticino represent a very limited outbreak. But the virus will return, and we will see larger outbreaks. It’s important that we’re prepared for this throughout Switzerland.” The Ticino health authorities, in collaboration with SUPSI and the Federal Office of Public Health (FOPH), have launched a surveillance program for the West Nile virus. The goal is to reduce the risk of new cases of West Nile virus; SUPSI’s Institute of Microbiology serves as the national center of expertise. Should the federal government take further action? Merlani: “The federal government should, if possible, support preventive measures against the tiger mosquito. Cases of dengue fever could also occur in the future. I would be more concerned about this disease than about chikungunya fever. The first infection with dengue fever is usually mild, but a second infection can be quite dangerous.” Lifestyle"The chikungunya cases in Ticino are a wake-up call for Switzerland" More About this Topic No flights from Zurich to the Maldives Beond Airlines is canceling all scheduled flights "Fraud" Customers are complaining about Kim Kardashian's $148 Advent calendar Paleontology Prehistoric whale with an extremely asymmetrical skull described Bibliography Bärfuss's "Mother's Book" Nominated for the 19th Swiss Book Prize Most Read 1China Blocks ABB's Sale of Its Robotics Division to SoftBank 2Cyberattack Shuts Down Thousands of Refrigerators in Western Europe 3Young singer gives concert without a headscarf - and defies the mullahs 4Iran Sets Seven Conditions for Talks with the U.S. 5Even months later, these questions about the fire disaster remain unanswered

Jharkhand sounds Dengue, Chikungunya alert during monsoon - News Arena India
新闻简报贾坎德邦政府发布了一项全州咨询,以应对季风季节登革热和基孔肯雅热的高风险。该通报于 2026 年 11 月 9 日发布,强调需要立即采取预防措施并加强 监测 以遏制这些蚊媒疾病的传播。卫生、医学教育和家庭福利部指示副专员重点关注伊蚊在死水中繁殖的脆弱和积水地区。常见的繁殖地点包括冷却器、未加盖的水箱、水桶和废弃的瓶子。政府已指示市政公司、地区管理机构和社区志愿者共同努力,发现并摧毁蚊子滋生地。卫生工作者的任务是检查居民区是否有幼虫并消除繁殖源。还要求各地区确保 监测 设备的可用性,例如塑料铲和幼虫收集瓶。敦促居民清洁储水容器,盖好头顶水箱,如果出现发烧症状,请立即就医。该建议强调了公众参与预防这些疾病传播的重要性,特别是在蚊子活动增加的雨季。
查看新闻五要素
时间
贾坎德邦政府于 2026 年 11 月 9 日在季风季节发布了警报。该警报是为了应对季风季节蚊媒疾病风险增加而发布的。
地点与对象
该建议已向该州所有地区发布,针对蚊子更容易滋生的脆弱和积水地区。
事件
贾坎德邦政府发布了一项全州范围的建议,以启动预防措施并加强 监测,以遏制登革热和基孔肯雅热(蚊媒疾病)的传播。该建议由卫生、医学教育和家庭福利部发布。
规模、影响与风险
该通报强调了季风季节登革热和基孔肯雅热的风险,这与雌性伊蚊的繁殖有关。政府已指示各地区政府重点关注这些领域,并制定详细的控制行动计划。
应对、状态与不确定性
贾坎德邦政府加强了对登革热和基孔肯雅热的防范,指示地区政府保持高度戒备。预防措施包括媒介 监测、识别和消除蚊子滋生地以及市政公司、地区管理机构和社区志愿者之间的协调。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Jharkhand sounds Dengue, Chikungunya alert during monsoon - News Arena India

News Brief
The Jharkhand government has issued a statewide advisory to address the heightened risk of dengue and chikungunya during the monsoon season. The advisory, published on November 9, 2026, emphasizes the need for immediate preventive measures and intensified surveillance to curb the spread of these mosquito-borne diseases. The Department of Health, Medical Education and Family Welfare directed Deputy Commissioners to focus on vulnerable and waterlogged areas where Aedes mosquitoes breed in stagnant water. Common breeding sites include coolers, uncovered tanks, buckets, and discarded bottles. The government has instructed municipal corporations, district administrations, and community volunteers to work together in detecting and destroying mosquito breeding sites. Health workers are tasked with inspecting residential areas for larvae and eliminating breeding sources. Districts have also been asked to ensure the availability of surveillance equipment such as plastic dippers and larva collection vials. Residents are urged to clean water storage containers, keep overhead tanks covered, and seek medical attention promptly if they develop fever symptoms. The advisory underscores the importance of public participation in preventing the spread of these diseases, especially during the rainy season when mosquito activity increases.

Follow us News Arena Network - Ranchi - UPDATED: July 13, 2026, 09:00 AM - 2 min read Every district has also been asked to prepare a detailed action plan and submit it to the state headquarters, with dengue and chikungunya control being treated as a priority. The Jharkhand government has stepped up its preparedness against dengue and chikungunya, directing all district administrations to remain on high alert as the monsoon season increases the risk of mosquito-borne diseases. The Department of Health, Medical Education and Family Welfare has issued a statewide advisory asking Deputy Commissioners to launch immediate preventive measures and intensify surveillance to curb the spread of infections. Additional Chief Secretary Ajay Kumar Singh has instructed district authorities to focus on vulnerable and waterlogged areas where mosquito breeding is more likely. He has asked officials to ensure that breeding sites are identified and eliminated on priority to prevent a rise in cases during the rainy season. The advisory notes that both dengue and chikungunya are viral illnesses transmitted through the bite of the female Aedes mosquito, which breeds in clean, stagnant water. Common breeding grounds include water stored in coolers, uncovered tanks, buckets, plastic containers, discarded bottles, old tyres and other items where rainwater accumulates. The department has stressed the need for regular cleaning of such locations and prompt removal of stagnant water. To strengthen the prevention drive, the Health Department has directed municipal corporations, municipal councils, district administrations, Multi-Purpose Workers (MPWs), Sahiyas and community volunteers to work in coordination to detect and destroy mosquito breeding sites. Every district has also been asked to prepare a detailed action plan and submit it to the state headquarters, with dengue and chikungunya control being treated as a priority. Door-to-door vector surveillance will form a major part of the campaign. Health workers will inspect residential areas to identify mosquito larvae and eliminate breeding sources. Districts have also been instructed to ensure the availability of essential surveillance equipment, including plastic dippers, pipettes and larva collection vials. The department has appealed to residents to play an active role in preventing the spread of the diseases by ensuring that water does not stagnate in or around their homes. People have been advised to clean coolers and water storage containers regularly, keep overhead tanks covered and seek medical attention at the nearest health facility if they develop fever, instead of resorting to self-medication. 提契诺州确认四例 基孔肯雅热 本地病例 - blue News

Four Local Cases of Chikungunya Fever Confirmed in Ticino - blue News
新闻简报2026年11月4日星期三,瑞士提契诺州卫生和社会事务部发布公告称,瑞士提契诺州确认了四例本地基孔肯雅热病例。由虎蚊传播的基孔肯雅病毒在瑞士首次出现本地传播。受影响的人最近没有去过病毒传播的地区,并且他们表现出良好的临床病程,持续的关节疼痛是主要症状。目前的公共风险被认为非常低,部分原因是病例数量较少且蚊子季节即将结束。提契诺州医疗官员办公室与 SUPSI、市政当局和联邦机构一起,在受影响地区启动了流行病学调查和蚊虫控制措施。这一发展标志着瑞士 传播 的首次本地化,凸显了持续保持警惕和采取预防措施的必要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
The Ticino Department of Health and Social Affairs announced this on Wednesday, 04.11.2026, 11:34 (N5).
地点与对象
这些病例在瑞士提契诺州(N2)确诊。
事件
瑞士提契诺州确诊了四例本地 基孔肯雅热 病例,病毒由虎蚊传播(N1、N3)。
规模、影响与风险
四名受影响的个体表现出良好的临床病程,持续的关节疼痛是主要症状。目前公众面临的风险被认为非常低,部分原因是病例数量较少且蚊子季节即将结束(N7、N9)。
应对、状态与不确定性
提契诺州医疗官员办公室与 SUPSI、市政当局和联邦机构合作,已在受影响地区 (N8) 启动流行病学调查和蚊虫控制措施。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Four Local Cases of Chikungunya Fever Confirmed in Ticino - blue News

News Brief
Four local cases of chikungunya fever were confirmed in Ticino, Switzerland, according to an announcement by the Ticino Department of Health and Social Affairs on Wednesday, November 4, 2026. The chikungunya virus, transmitted by the tiger mosquito, has been locally spread for the first time in Switzerland. The affected individuals had not recently traveled to areas where the virus is circulating, and they showed a favorable clinical course, with persistent joint pain being the main symptom. The current public risk is considered very low, partly due to the small number of cases and the approaching end of the mosquito season. The Office of the Ticino Cantonal Medical Officer, along with SUPSI, municipal authorities, and federal agencies, has launched epidemiological investigations and mosquito control measures in the affected areas. This development marks the first local transmission of the chikungunya virus in Switzerland, highlighting the need for continued vigilance and preventive actions.

感染基孔肯雅热的人住在同一条街上 - 蓝色新闻

The people who have contracted chikungunya fever live on the same street - blue News
新闻简报在瑞士提契诺州,有四人感染了 基孔肯雅热,他们都住在同一社区。州医疗官员乔治·梅拉尼 (Giorgio Merlani) 指出,其中两名患者可能被同一只虎蚊叮咬,而其他患者则可能被该蚊子的后代叮咬。作为回应,在受影响的地区喷洒了一种杀死成年虎蚊的物质。医务人员强调,这一措施是有效的,因为虎蚊一生的飞行距离通常不到100米。本报告发布日期为2026年11月4日,该事件目前正在调查中。传播 的风险与虎蚊有关,已知虎蚊会传播病毒。该回应强调了控制 暴发 并防止进一步传播的持续努力。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

The people who have contracted chikungunya fever live on the same street - blue News

News Brief
In Ticino, Switzerland, four individuals have contracted chikungunya fever, all living in the same neighborhood. The cantonal medical officer, Giorgio Merlani, noted that it is possible two of the patients were bitten by the same tiger mosquito, while the others were likely bitten by a descendant of that mosquito. In response, a substance that kills adult tiger mosquitoes was sprayed in the affected area. The medical officer emphasized that this measure is effective, as tiger mosquitoes typically travel less than 100 meters in their lifetime. The publication date of this report is November 4, 2026, and the event is currently under investigation. The risk of transmission is linked to the tiger mosquito, which is known to spread the virus. The response highlights the ongoing efforts to control the outbreak and prevent further spread.

In collaboration with the University of Applied Sciences and Arts of Ticino (SUPSI), a substance that kills adult tiger mosquitoes was sprayed into the air in the affected individuals’ neighborhood after it was detected in their blood, explained Ticino’s cantonal medical officer, Giorgio Merlani. Time
The event date is not explicitly stated in the evidence, but the publication date is 2026-11-04. The evidence mentions that the four patients were identified in the neighborhood, and the medical officer's response was dated 04.11.2026.
Location and population
The affected individuals are located in Ticino, a region in Switzerland. The event involves four people who contracted chikungunya fever and live in the same neighborhood.
Event
Four individuals in Ticino, Switzerland, contracted chikungunya fever, and they all reside in the same neighborhood. The cantonal medical officer noted that it is possible two of the four patients were bitten by the same mosquito, while the others were likely bitten by a descendant of the first one.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak involves four cases of chikungunya fever. The risk is associated with the tiger mosquito, which can transmit the virus. The medical officer mentioned that a substance to kill adult tiger mosquitoes was sprayed in the neighborhood as a response.
Response, status and uncertainty
A substance to kill adult tiger mosquitoes was sprayed in the affected neighborhood. The medical officer's statement indicates that the investigation is ongoing, and the response is based on the detection of the virus in the patients' blood.
